# Interpretation of the Saying of Anaxagoras <note>Anaxagoras (c. 500–428 BC) was a Pre-Socratic Greek philosopher who taught that "Mind" (Nous) was the primary cause of physical changes.</note>

God is from the beginning of all things; that is true, for God is the **Light** as the **Fire**; therefore, God in His essence is compared to fire. Fire is a beginning of all things that grow and are capable of change <note>original: "wächsllichen und fählichen Dinge." This refers to things that are mutable, subject to growth, and capable of receiving new forms.</note>. It is just the same in our **Art** <term>Art: (original: Kunst) The alchemical practice, specifically the "Great Work" of transforming matter.</term>, for the beginning
<margin>B</margin>
is the **Fire**, as the warmth drives **Nature** so that she begins to work. And in the **Operation** <note>The specific alchemical procedure or laboratory process.</note>, **Body**, **Spirit**, and **Soul** <note>original: "Corpus, Spiritus & Anima." These are the three prime principles of alchemical theory, often identified with Salt, Mercury, and Sulfur.</note> reveal themselves. That is to say: as Earth and Water; for the Earth is the **Body** <note>original: "Corpus"</note>, the **Oil** is the **Soul** <note>original: "Anima"</note>, and the **Water** is the **Spirit** <note>original: "Spiritus"</note> or ghost. 

This occurs through the **Mildness** <note>original: "Mildigkeit." In this context, it implies God's nourishing grace or generative mercy.</note> and **Goodness** of God, without which Nature can bring nothing to pass, as the **Philosopher** <note>Likely referring to Anaxagoras, though "The Philosopher" often refers to Aristotle in medieval texts.</note> himself says: God's Mildness governs all things. And beneath the thickness of the **Earth**, after the **Creation**, Mildness and **Uprightness** <note>original: "Redlichkeit." This suggests the integrity, purity, or "honesty" of a substance—its tendency to remain true to its nature without volatile deception.</note> reveal themselves. By "beneath the thickness of the Earth," the Philosopher means when the Earth is separated from the Water (in that beginning which is called the **Creation**) and is **dissolved** <term>Dissolved: (original: solvirt) The process of 'solutio,' where a solid is reduced to its liquid essence.</term> into **Oil** and **Water** within itself. Then the **Oil** is the Uprightness, and the **Water** gives the **Soul** to the Oil together with the **Body**. Within this is the Mildness; and the Oil is upright, for it remains always with the **Body** and takes no more unto itself than is given to it from **Heaven**—that is, from the **Water**—and the Water

# Interpretation of the Saying of Pythagoras.

The Philosopher <note>Pythagoras, the ancient Greek philosopher and mathematician, who was highly revered in alchemical traditions as a master of hidden wisdom.</note> says: what that might be, which one feels and does not see, and does not know—by this the Philosopher means it is a thing of our Art <note>The "Art" is the standard term for Alchemy itself.</note>, which is prepared from our Art, and is a thing that one feels and does not see. That is to be understood thus: one feels it, but one does not see it, and one does not know its effect; but whoever knows it and yet does not know its effect, he shall nevermore arrive at the Art. For there is nothing more than one single thing in the whole world from which the Art is made and worked, and that thing comes from a dark place where it is nevermore seen, and its effect and virtue <note>In early science and alchemy, "virtue" (Tugend) refers to the inherent power or medicinal property of a substance.</note> are by no... <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "man" indicates the next page likely begins with "man erkannt" (one recognized/known).</note>

<summary>This page concludes an explanation of the necessity of unifying the alchemical Body, Spirit, and Oil to achieve the "white and red" stages of perfection, then introduces a riddle by Pythagoras regarding the singular, hidden source of the Alchemical Art.</summary>
<keywords>Corpus, Spiritus, Solution, Albedo, Rubedo, Pythagoras, The Art, The One Thing, Alchemical Elements</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Interpretation of the Saying of Pythagoras" from page 120. It expands on the symbolic identification of the "Small World" (the human being) as the source of the alchemical Matter, rather than literal earth or mountains.</meta>

->❃ ❃ ❃<-

...nobody recognizes it, except only the sages of this Art; also in the Matter <term>Materia: The base substance or "First Matter" used at the start of the alchemical process.</term> the great secret is hidden, just as the Air and the Fire—that is, the Sun <term>Sol: In alchemy, the Sun represents Gold and the masculine principle.</term> and Moon <term>Luna: Represents Silver and the feminine principle.</term>, and Stars—are concealed within it, and yet are invisible. As the Philosopher <note>Pythagoras.</note> says: that which one does not see and does not recognize is Heaven alone, but that which one feels and does not see is the Earth. By this the Philosopher means that the Earth is the density or Body <term>Corpus: The physical, solid substance or "salt" that remains at the bottom of a vessel.</term> which is found at the base of the Matter, which has gathered together in the Matter; that, one can feel and recognize. 

And where the Philosopher further says that there is something between Heaven and Earth which one does not recognize—that is "in the world"—the Philosopher means by this that the Matter of our Stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone.</note> is found in the world, specifically in the "Small World" <note>original: "Minori Mundo." This refers to the Microcosm, the ancient philosophical belief that the human being is a miniature map of the entire universe (the Macrocosm).</note>, and not in the wild rocky mountains or in the soil, but especially between Heaven and Earth, in the air, as in the Small World. Furthermore, when he says that therein is integrity and the senses—such as smelling, tasting, hearing, touching—the Philosopher means that within Man there is integrity (specifically the rational soul) and sense, for Man can recognize and reflect upon these things, and can touch, feel, and understand them. 

With this, he gives us an indication and a focus on where our Stone is found: namely through seeing, hearing, smelling, tasting, and grasping. Through seeing, we recognize that the Matter of our Stone is thick, thin, clear, and orderly...

<summary>This page provides a symbolic interpretation of Pythagoras’s teachings, explaining that the alchemical "Matter" is not found in nature or mountains, but within the "Microcosm" or the human being. It emphasizes using the five senses and human reason to identify the hidden celestial qualities (Sun, Moon, and Stars) within the physical "Body" of the substance.</summary>

...one can handle, also so that you know when it is or becomes black, white, and red <note>These represent the three primary stages of the Great Work: Nigredo (blackness/decay), Albedo (whiteness/purification), and Rubedo (redness/perfection).</note>, and the like. Through smelling, one recognizes the foul odor from the good, such as when it has moved past its initial stench and is transformed into the clearest and purest form. In tasting, [one knows] when it has changed from sharpness and bitterness to a noble and sweet flavor. In feeling, [one discerns] whether the Stone is hard, soft, ripe, or mature <note>original: "zeitig"</note>, and whether it is coarse or subtle. This must happen in this Art, and for this, the senses and reason must be used so that one recognizes one thing before another, such as Earth and Water, or black, white, and red. This is done through <term>Distillation: Purifying a liquid by heating it into vapor and condensing it back into a liquid.</term>, <term>Putrefaction: The process of "rotting" or breaking down the matter to release its essence.</term>, <term>Solution: Dissolving a solid into a liquid.</term>, <term>Coagulation: The thickening or solidifying of a liquid.</term>, <term>Imbibition: Repeatedly "feeding" or soaking a solid with liquid.</term>, <term>Fermentation: Adding a "yeast" to the matter to allow it to multiply in quality.</term>, and <term>Tincturing: The final stage where the Stone gains the power to transform other substances.</term> All of this is recognized through the senses, one after the other; and our Stone has its origin through the senses, and each individual labor occurs in 46 days <note>Alchemical timelines of 46 days are often symbolic, sometimes linked to the 46 years it took to build the Temple of Jerusalem.</note>.

## Interpretation of the Saying of Par-
## menides.

Know that the envious <note>original: "gehäßigen," which in an alchemical context refers to the "Envious Philosophers" (Invidiosi) who were said to intentionally hide their secrets from the unworthy.</note> **Philosophers** spoke of many waters, of many stones, metals, and minerals, because they wished to deceive you; by this, he means they covered it up so that it should not be revealed to all people. Therefore, they have written of many stones, metals, and minerals, and those who wish to work according to the literal letter of the writing—even though the **Philosophers** wrote rightly and truly—will nonetheless be deceived. This happens because they want to work yet have no foundation or knowledge of the Art; thus, it is not the fault of the **Philosophers**, but of their own ignorance. For the **Philosophers** wrote of stones, and they *are* stones, but dug from *our* mountains; if they write of metals, they are metals, but smelted from *our* ore; if they write of waters, they are waters, but fetched from *our* well and from *our* sea.

<summary>This page describes the final stage of creating the Philosopher's Stone through fermentation and explains that alchemical terms like "stones" and "metals" refer to specific, secret substances ("Our" materials) rather than common literal ones.</summary>

When they write of **Sulfur**, it is sulfur, white and red, but separated from our **ore**; when they write of **Salt**, it is also salt, but drawn from our salt spring. For this is our <term>Sun: [Sol] The alchemical name for gold or the masculine, heating principle.</term>, our verdigris, saltpeter, our <term>Salt: [Sal] One of the three primary principles (with Sulfur and Mercury), representing the physical body.</term>, alkali, our <term>orpiment: [Auripigment] A deep orange-yellow arsenic sulfide mineral once used as a pigment.</term>, our arsenic, our poison, our medicine, and the like, without number. Even if they named all the things in the whole world, it is still our "thing," and however many names they give it, it is still only a single thing. Therefore, the **Philosophers** write correctly, but those who seek and do not find cannot understand this; they also do not know the <term>operation: [Operation] The specific laboratory procedure or chemical process.</term> of this thing. That is why the **Philosophers** say: "Abandon <note>original: "verlasset Solem, Lunam, Venerem, Saturnum." The author is advising the reader to stop looking at common metals like gold (Sun), silver (Moon), copper (Venus), and lead (Saturn).</note> **Gold, Silver, Copper, and Lead** for the sake of our ore." By this, the **Philosopher** means that in all metals and earthly things, it is not found in our ore alone <note>The author suggests that the "secret" substance is hidden within all matter, not just the literal ores of these metals.</note>; and where the **Philosopher** concludes and says, "It is all the labor of Nature, and yet becomes Nature"—whoever understands this well is a blessed **Artist**, and is duty-bound to thank God.

This is <term>Sulfur: [Sulphur] In alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, masculine, and "combustible" principle within the matter.</term> joined to Sulfur, and it is its own property, just as the <term>Spirit: [Spiritus] The volatile, active principle that gives life or movement to the matter.</term> belongs to the <term>Body: [Corpus] The solid, physical shell or base of the substance.</term>, and the Body to the Spirit, for one cannot do without the other. And when the Spirit is given to the Body in an orderly way, from time to time, so that it gradually <term>coagulates: [coagulirt] To solidify or thicken a liquid into a fixed, stable state.</term> into the Body, then the Sulfur is joined to Sulfur and is entirely perfect. For no foreign thing enters into it—it is its own property—therefore it accepts nothing except its own Spirit. For the Body contains within itself the <term>Soul: [Seel] The mediating life-force that binds the volatile Spirit to the solid Body.</term> of the Body, which was drawn out of the Body, and this must be returned to it alone; therefore, nothing is so fitting or pleasing to it as its own Soul and its own Body—that is, its own nature.

For this reason, the <term>Philosopher: [Philosophus] A reference to an ancient authority, likely Hermes Trismegistus or Geber.</term> says: "And when these are thus brought together—the Spirit to the Body, the Sulfur to Sulfur, the Earth to Water—and they have become as white as a pearl, then the Body will hold the Spirit within itself, so that it cannot fly away <note>In alchemy, 'flying away' refers to volatility or evaporation. Once a substance is 'fixed,' it remains stable and does not evaporate when heated.</note> or ever be separated from it again." And thus you have the Earth of the Philosophers <note>original: "Terram Philosophorum"</note>, purified to the highest whiteness, in which we sow our grain so that it may bring forth fruit without number.

Ausle- <note>This is a "catchword," a fragment of the first word on the next page (likely "Auslegung" or "Explanation"), included to help the printer or reader maintain the correct order of pages.</note>

<summary>This page describes the "Albedo" or white stage of the alchemical work, emphasizing the necessity of reuniting the separated Spirit and Body into a single, stable substance called the Earth of the Philosophers.</summary>
<keywords>Sulfur, Spirit, Body, Coagulation, Soul, Albedo, Earth of the Philosophers, fixation, purification</keywords>

## Interpretation of the Proverb of Pythagoras.

You may well marvel at the strange and wondrous sayings of the philosophers, and how diversely they have described this thing; yet they agree on one point, and all their sayings tend toward one goal. If you pay close attention, you will learn the truth, as the <term>Philosopher: [Philosophus] A general term for ancient alchemical authorities, often referring to Hermes Trismegistus or, in this context, Pythagoras.</term> says: "We give you a <term>rule: [Regiment] In alchemy, this refers to a 'regimen' or a specific set of instructions for governing the heat and stages of the chemical process.</term> of these things: that it be water, dry as the **Water of the Moon** which you have prepared." By this, the Philosopher means that when we wish to work this Art, we must govern ourselves strictly according to understanding. Where we fail and miss the mark, we might rightly scold the philosophers as deceivers—for they provide the rule openly—but if you do not succeed, it is the fault of your own cleverness or folly, for you interpret the writings according to your own mad heads differently than they are written.

Furthermore, as the Philosopher says, "that it drinks its own water," he means that when the dry is separated from the moist, the extracted water of the <term>Body: [Corpus] The solid, physical matter or base of the alchemical substance.</term> is true water; and it is the <term>Water of the Moon: [Aqua Lunae] A poetic name for the purified, silver-like liquid state of the matter, often associated with the white stage of the process.</term> which you have prepared through <term>Putrefaction: [Putrifaction] The stage where matter is broken down and decayed (often turning black) to release its hidden spirit.</term> and <term>Distillation: [Distillation] The process of purifying a liquid by heating it into vapor and then condensing it back into liquid.</term>. For the extracted water is reckoned as the husband, and the earth—acting as the <term>Body</term>—as the wife. Since the husband has become water in himself, you must [return] the water from the husband to

<meta>The catchword "dem" (to the) indicates the sentence continues on the next page, likely reading "to the wife" (dem Weib) to complete the metaphor of the alchemical marriage.</meta>

<summary>This page explains that the confusing metaphors used by alchemists all point to a single truth regarding the "Water of the Moon." It defines the symbolic relationship between the extracted liquid (husband) and the solid earth (wife) during the processes of putrefaction and distillation.</summary>

# Another little treatise, which is of the same content as the previous one, very useful to read.

## Preface.

Everyone might well wonder why the philosophers have devised so many hidden sayings in honor of this divine and most precious art, called the **Secret of the Philosophers** <note>original: *Secretum Philosophorum*</note>, in order to hide it from the unworthy. This, however, produces nothing fruitful among the **disciples** <note>original: *Discipuln*</note> of these **secrets** <term>Arcana|From the Latin 'arcanum,' meaning a deep secret or mystery; in alchemy, it refers to the hidden transformative powers of nature.</term>, since the authors have busied themselves with hiding the truth through such language. And when the unwise read these writings of theirs and hear what great wealth, virtue, and power our art can bring forth, it makes a very sweet and lovely sound in their ears. They begin to imagine they are sitting on golden chairs and that heaven is hung with the music of harps <note>original: *und hange der Himmel voller Saitenspiel*; a German idiom suggesting a state of idealized, perhaps unrealistic, happiness or high expectations.</note>. From this springs a great eagerness and desire, so that they imagine they will quickly become a **Doctor** and wish to perform great **miracles** <note>original: *Miracul*</note>. Nor do they think they will err, or that the philosophers might deceive them. They do not know, however, that the wise have hidden the foundation of this art from the very beginning and have explained the truth only to their "children" <note>The "Children of the Art" or "Children of Wisdom" is a traditional term for initiated alchemists.</note> in **parables** and allegories.

<summary>This page introduces a new treatise on alchemy, warning that philosophers purposefully used cryptic language and parables to hide the "Secret of the Philosophers" from the unworthy and the naive.</summary>

Yet there are many craftsmen who neither understand nor wish to notice this; they work without reason and are lost in their thoughts, not knowing whether their art follows nature or not—which they then wish to improve or increase. The highly renowned philosopher Arnoldus <note>Referring to Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a celebrated physician and alchemical authority.</note> says of them that they approach the art like a donkey to its manger, not knowing where it sticks its head. They seek to accomplish the works of nature, and when the work is finished, they see well enough what they find. Refers to the successful completion of the Great Work or the attainment of the Philosopher's Stone.</term>, or desires to become a partaker of this most noble art, he must know from what <term>Seed|German: *Saamen*. In alchemy, 'seed' refers to the essential spark or 'life force' within matter that allows it to grow and transform.</term> such bodies of metals are born by nature within the earth. With this, we can then—in a short time—make the impure and imperfect bodies of both humans and metals pure and perfect. 

We must extract this seed from the perfect, pure, and mature <note>German: *zeitigen*; meaning 'ripe' or 'fully developed'.</note> bodies, if we are to reach the desired end in any other way. So that every investigator of this noble art may the better and sooner come to the knowledge of nature, I have set down the following **Treatise on the Beginning of Nature, the Creation, and the Generation of Man**, which a disciple and follower of our mastery should read through well, consider, and take to heart; then he will certainly not wander astray from the right path.

# The Fear of the Lord is the Be-
## ginning of Wisdom.

Indeed, all the wise men and chosen philosophers have exerted themselves to first learn to recognize Almighty God from His wondrous creations, as much as has been possible for human reason. This they were able to accomplish or fathom by no other means or path than by diligently contemplating the **beginning of all natural things**. This they did earnestly, and thereby recognized God as the Highest. For they found within natural things the omnipotence of the Creator, and also what great virtue, operation, power, and secrecy <note>German: *Heimlichkeit*; in this context, it refers to the hidden mysteries or occult properties of nature.</note> the eternal God has instilled into Nature. Therefore, they reflected well and took it to heart how they might bring such secrets to light with great wisdom for the benefit of the human race.

For this reason, they first reveal the beginning of all natural things, and primarily the arrival of the first human, and in turn his passing away, speaking thus:

When Almighty God in the beginning, out of the subtle liquid <note>original: *Liquore*</note> or imperceptible mist—the form of which was neither moist, nor dry, nor cold, nor

When Adam had been thus informed by God, he also kept it in great secrecy and silence from his children; yet finally, he obtained permission from the Most High God to reveal it to his son Seth before his end. He instructed and taught him the *Preparation of the Stone* <note>original: "Praeparatio lapidis"</note>. And if the poor man Adam had not known such a high mystery and great concealment of nature, and had not possessed the *Philosophers' Stone* <note>original: "Lapidem Philosophorum"</note>, he would not have lived 300 years, much less 900 and more, since he was troubled, and the sins he had committed drove him, and the curse of God tormented his conscience.

[the] <note>completing the thought from the previous page: "the [mother's]..."</note> Matrix's whiteness [drawn] out of the blackness, that is, drawn from the **Body** <note>original: *Corpore*; the solid physical matter in the flask</note>, and nothing more is needed here than patience and delay, as Morienus <note>Morienus: A legendary 7th-century hermit and alchemist often cited in medieval texts as a master of patience and natural processes.</note> speaks of it. This coagulated white water is the true **White Foliated Earth** <note>original: *Terra alba Foliata*; a crucial alchemical stage where the matter becomes white and flaky, signaling the success of the "whitening" or *albedo* phase</note>, in which the Philosopher tells us to sow our gold and silver, so that they multiply with a hundred and thousandfold fruit; and [it is] the clear fountain, of which the Count of Trevisan <note>original: *Comes Trevisanus*; Bernard of Trevisan (1406–1490), a famous alchemist known for his allegorical stories</note> makes mention, into which the King enters to bathe. And when the King then sits in the bath, none of his servants should come in to him, but they should keep the King's clothes in safekeeping, until such time as the King has dried up the entire fountain; then the King will make all his servants into lords, so that they will be as powerful in authority, power, and dominion, as well as strength and might, as the King himself was when he first went into the fountain. But the King is now adorned with threefold dignity more than he was before, for he wears a triple crown on his head, and wears a ruby-red garment, beneath which is the shirt of purity girded with the band of justice, and is thus an honorable King of Life, whose dominion no man in this world can conceive; therefore one should provide him with a pure, chaste bride, who shall be born from his own tribe, so that he

[so that] <note>completing the thought from page 164: "...for he wears [new garments so that]..."</note> she might recognize him, and be able to work within her, so that many noble children may be born of them both. The **Redness** is hidden and contained within the **Whiteness**; one should not extract it, but rather cook it gently until it becomes perfectly red. And regarding this Whiteness, the *Turba* <note>original: *Turba Philosophorum*; "The Assembly of the Philosophers," one of the most famous and oldest Latin alchemical texts, written as a dialogue between ancient sages</note> speaks thus: "When you seekers find the **Whiteness** after the **Blackness**, then be certain that the **Redness** follows the **Whiteness**; for the Redness is hidden within the Whiteness. One must not extract it, but cook it gently until it becomes red." With this, an end.

The love of God and of one's neighbor is the perfection of all wisdom.

### Hermes speaks:

It is necessary to have knowledge of the beginning, of both natural and artistic <note>original: *kunstreichen*; in this context, "Art" refers specifically to the "Hermetic Art" or alchemy, contrasting natural processes with those assisted by human skill</note> things; for he who does not know the right beginning will also not reach the end.

The love of God and of one's neighbor is the perfection of all wisdom. To love God is the highest wisdom, and time is our possession. <note>original: "Diligere Deum sapientia Summa, et Tempus possessio nostra."</note>
